Age: 35Breakout moment: The Cannes hit Club Kid, which he wrote, directed, and starred in as a party promoter navigating unexpected parenthood.—————

I still do, yeah, at the phone call scene at the end. But it feels very distant from me. I feel like I’m watching it more as an observer now, which is nice, and I feel different than when I made it. I was such an open wound.

Cannes was such an emotional experience. I’m 35, and I’ve always known that I had something to say and I knew how to say it, but I’ve never been given the opportunity. So, honestly, to feel like I was being seen and understood was like 20 years of validation that I didn’t get. And so something shifted, that week, in me. I’ve noticed myself not having to use humor as a shield anymore, as much. I’m less afraid to be vulnerable—and not just in my public life but in my personal life too.

Of course, you shouldn’t need the validation of anyone to feel like you’re worthy, but let’s be real.…

What gagged me this year? I would say, honestly, as a statement of ambition, the Javis’ movie [La Bola Negra]. You marvel at it because you’re like, “How the fuck did they pull this off?” To even want to tell that complex of a story, and to just do it and go balls to the wall, is very inspiring.

I was like, “This is too long. It’s embarrassing now.” —A.P.

Jacket and pants by Canali. Shirt by Amiri. Boots by Alessandro Vasini. Tie by Husbands.

Age: 41Breakout moment: Reporting on how the Steve Ballmer–owned Clippers were allegedly funny with money, on his podcast Pablo Torre Finds Out, leading to one of the most severe team penalties in sports history.—————

Two days ago, I walked into this event that The New York Times was holding…it was like the 175th birthday party [for the paper]. I walk out the elevator and literally the first person I see is [NBA commissioner] Adam Silver. I won’t say exactly what he told me, but the tenor of our conversation was like, This is the HBO series adaptation of what this moment would be.

As a first-generation American—my parents are from the Philippines—there’s always been a dream of mine to be cast as a white person. So…Andrew Garfield, come on. Let’s do this.

The funny part is that I’m not even done reporting this story yet. The federal government is now investigating the Clippers, and so I’m now investigating the government’s investigation in the era of Trump too, which is like its own rabbit hole inside of a rabbit hole, which is fucking wild. But at the same time, it feels like now our number is listed in the phone book…actual other NBA owners are calling me up saying, “Can you look into this?”

It’s hard not to be sappy about this, but the lesson I learned is that if you’re telling the truth, you can walk into any room and feel like that as a suit of armor. Even when the person you’re investigating is one of the 10 richest people in the world. —F.T.

Coat by Michael Kors. Vintage pajamas from Palace Costume Co. Socks by London Sock Company. Glasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklaces (throughout), his own.

Age: 37Breakout moments: Stealing scenes as a ’70s Provincetown line cook in the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony, and his stand-up special Uncle Stav.—————

I just did The Drew Barrymore Show. That experience was so magical. I was so nervous to be around Drew Barrymore. I’ve done a lot of cool shit, and that’s the one where I was like, “I am actually nervous to the point where I’m not a professional. I’m just a stammering little boy who’s with the girl he had a crush on from The Wedding Singer when he was a fat 10-year-old, and I’m trying to keep it together.”

Last week I allowed myself an aimlessly wander around New York City day. Got a little stoned, walked around, stumbled into a movie. Walked along the water on the West Side. Sat down to a little random omakase. Rode a Citi Bike around. I call it the hero’s journey, where you leave your home at, whatever, 11 a.m. ,with just the bare provisions, and you’re not allowed to go home. You just have to keep moving and keep doing stuff.

I filmed this Judd Apatow movie The Comeback King, with Glen Powell, Cristin Milioti, Madelyn Cline. That comes out in February. I’m excited to do stand-up—for fun, not to explicitly work on another hour. I’m excited to go back to clubs and just like, “Can I be the funniest person in this club tonight?” And that’s my only goal.

To be honest with you, the last three years, it’s [been] all go. But you got to have these big years, or else GQ doesn’t talk to you at the end of the year. —A.P.

On Halkias: Jacket and pants by Brooks Brothers. Shirt by Many. Shoes by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Sunglasses by Valention. On Torre: Jacket by Tom Ford. Shirt by Factor’s. Pants by Louis Vuitton Men’s. Shoes by G.H. Bass. Sunglasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Ring, his own.

The DJ played “Look Like My Mama” in the strip club. You ain’t never heard Tyler, the Creator get played in a strip club. We crossing worlds that’s never been crossed before.

The day before the album came out, we were in Area 29 in Houston, doing our thing. Regular night, it ain’t too packed, throwing money. They tap me on my shoulder like, “My boy want to holler at you.” They take me upstairs to this private section, pull the curtain back…it’s Travis Scott.

It was epic, bro. N-gga came out from behind the curtain like Batman. The light hid half his face. He said, “I fuck with everything you got going on.” We go back down to that section, 15 minutes pass, then one of the owners came and grabbed me: “Look, look, look.” Travis was up there, playing [Chike’s song] “Packed Up.” —F.T.

Age: 27Breakout moment: Being a dead ringer for Sly Stallone in I Play Rocky, the film about…Sly Stallone playing Rocky.—————

In the thick of prep. I was training at Gleason’s Gym with Stephan James, who plays Carl Weathers. We were beating the crap out of each other and figuring out how to box. I had gained a bunch of weight, and I was trying to actually cut a few pounds, so I was in those sweatsuits, like a garbage-bag type thing. On the way to the gym, I would listen to Stallone and on the way home.… Everything I could find up to 1980, 1985, every piece of Stallone footage that existed.

I [asked] Seth Rogen, “Dude, how do you handle this stuff?” And he said, “Just go with the flow.” It was such a simple answer, but from somebody like him, I was like, He’s got a good grip on this. The simplicity of it landed.

Philip Seymour Hoffman is one. Chadwick Boseman. Christian Bale, Heath Ledger.

[The first Rocky] is the best movie—but when I was younger, the one that caught my eye was four. Rocky IV is nuts. —F.T.

Ages: Ambrossi, 42 | Calvo, 35Breakout moment: Cowriting and directing La Bola Negra, the gay Spanish historical epic loosely based on an unfinished Federico García Lorca play.——————————————

Javier Ambrossi: Very important. We understood that sometimes people expect LGBTQ+ stories to be niche or for a specific audience. We wanted to do a queer story about universal love.

Ambrossi: It’s a way to put in context how different we are. We change a lot, and the story of our grandparents and our story is very different.

Javier Calvo: What do we do with the freedom our ancestors fought for, and died for?

Calvo: Isabelle Huppert. For me, she’s everything.

Ambrossi: I will go with Julia Roberts. My Best Friend’s Wedding is a very important queer story. The relationship between a woman and a gay man—it was the first time I saw that onscreen. And Rupert Everett was so bold, and I could tell in his eyes that he was gay. I was a kid and I was like, “He’s not faking it.”

How did you celebrate when you won best director at Cannes?

Ambrossi: I put the award on the table and went to sleep. And then Javi came over at 8 a.m.…

Ambrossi: “Give me the award!” And he took it! —A.P.

“It’s Always About Me and What I Need or What I Want to See Out in the World”: Rick Owens on Reuniting With Adidas

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

“Everything I do is completely selfish,” says Rick Owens about his new Adidas collaboration, which is the latest in his off-and-on relationship with the athleticwear mega-brand. “It’s always about me and what I need or what I want to see out in the world.”

Owens is beaming in from Claridge’s hotel in London, where he was delayed because he had trouble connecting to Wi-Fi. He’s there because his longtime partner Michèle Lamy is a guest judge on RuPaul’s Drag Race. When he does appear on my screen, his face is all sharp angles framed by his signature jet-black hair parted down the middle and hanging pin straight; his nails are painted (black, of course) and on his long, equine nose is perched a pair of oversized, architectural glasses.

Owens is much like you: he suddenly knows a lot of runners. In fact, many of them are part of his “family,” a phrase he lovingly uses to describe those who work with him or are fans of the brand he’s nurtured for the better part of three decades. “So when I see them suit up and put on a branded trainer that’s not ours, it just feels like, ‘oh, we’ve got a hole.’ I mean, this is the hole that we need to fill.”

So Owens is back at it with Adidas, this time leveraging their springblade technology, a series of futuristic blades at the sole of the shoe. Owens has applied them to a gaiter-like boot and runner, and they have a characteristic futuristic, avant-garde look. “Having the opportunity to use somebody else’s technology—when they’re the best at what they do—and to be able to make runners … and to be able to get my team out of their other branded fucking sneakers and into ours, that was my motivation,” Owens said with his usual candor. “And we’re able to do it in a way that everybody can engage.”

The shoes were first revealed back in June during Owens’s runway show for the spring 2027 collection, and their dramatic, Brutalist silhouette was the perfect complement to his lean, somewhat regal ready-to-wear designs. It borrowed from his existing “Taco” series, named so as it's essentially a folded piece of fabric over the foot, yielding a deflated bag shape. “The crumpled volume feels careless and opulent at the same time,” Owens said, before adding, “at the same time, I’m half-Mexican and love tacos.”

Indeed, the shoes were just a small part of the larger Adidas collaboration, which included ready-to-wear, like sweeping hooded capes, tracksuit pants in lean and oversized silhouettes, sharp-shouldered sleeveless tunics, and jackets and bodysuits that were inflated using internal fans. The result was a mix between those blow-up Halloween costumes and what happens to Violet Beauregarde when she turns into a blueberry in Charlie and the Chocolate Factory. “I always think about how can I do a runway show that is impactful without being wasteful,” Owens said. “Without building sets that are going to be knocked down and thrown away. That’s wasteful.”

“The idea of using air and garments just fits into that,” he continued. “It’s something so pure and simple—and blending technology with something so naive, there was such charm in that. Inflated garments have been around for a long time. I mean, the Japanese workers have been using them for a long time, but then Adidas took it up, I don’t know, like 20 years ago or something. But then I found that in their archives, and so I thought, ‘Well, that’s timely.’”

And, yes, Rick-heads, those inflatable pieces will be produced for purchase. “I never wanted my runways to be an impossible dream,” he says. “In the old days, you would see runway shows with these women as these fantastic Thanksgiving Day parade floats, just these huge concoctions, and then the designer would come out at the end in jeans and a T-shirt. It deflated everything to me! It told me that the designer doesn’t believe in what he’s doing, and he’s not living it. It’s like saving your china for Sunday, or for special occasions. I want to be able to make stuff that people can have access to, and that is part of their everyday lives. So yeah, sure, there’s theater and exaggeration in what I do, but it’s connected to real life or my perception of it, which is more exaggerated than a lot of people, granted, but it’s for sale.”

While Owens is, let’s say, skeptical of the proliferation of collaborations—“If I see another collab, I’m going to roll my eyes until I fall over,” he said—the reason his with Adidas works is that they clearly give him the creative freedom to do as he pleases and it comes from a real place. It’s no big secret that Owens, with his lean physique and his at-home gym, is somewhat obsessed with working out. “When I changed my body in my 30s, when I took steroids, and started working out, it satisfied any kind of vanity the clothes would have been for. It was so much more profound than putting on an outfit. It was changing your entire bone structure. It was changing the foundation that the clothes were going to be on.”

Today his go-to gym outfit is pretty simple though—baggy shorts and thin T-shirts, and, when he’s traveling, he’ll often just wear hotel slippers. “I don’t really wear sneakers,” he says. “I’m just always in platforms.” Gym shoes, he says, take up too much room in his carry-on.

“So with Adidas, that’s my next step—to make a good workout gym shoe,” he said. “Super light and flat.”

Rick Owens for Adidas springblade shoes will range from $590 to $850 and will be available for purchase on November 17th at adidas.com, rickowens.eu, and select retailers.

Vogue Etiquette: Kelly Cutrone and Bozoma Saint John Have Very Different Ideas About How to Behave at Work

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

In 1948, Vogue published its 658-page Book of Etiquette, compiled by editor Millicent Fenwick, featuring how-tos, dos and don’ts, and the proper politesse for a remarkably varied set of scenarios. But as Fenwick writes in the introduction: “Etiquette is based on tradition, and yet it can change.” Some 75 years later, Oh, Behave! is a new monthly Vogue column in which experts sound off on today’s ever-evolving social etiquette.

There are two kinds of bosses: those who believe in work-life balance, and those who consider the very phrase a red flag. Kelly Cutrone, fashion’s legendary ball-breaker and founder of People’s Revolution, belongs firmly to the latter camp. Bozoma Saint John, a former marketing executive at Apple Music, Uber, and Netflix, is a little more forgiving. She’ll let you leave the office before your boss. She might even approve of wearing shorts to work.

For this installment of Oh, Behave!, we asked these two formidable women for a little workplace wisdom. Cutrone has lately been busy with everything from Farm to Fashion, a documentary about sheep and sustainable fashion, to Grindr’s Halloween festivities, while Saint John has brought her larger-than-life personality to The Real Housewives of Beverly Hills. From carrying a Birkin to an entry-level interview to answering late-night emails from your boss, we put the same workplace dilemmas to both women. Their answers are occasionally in agreement, frequently at odds, and never short on opinions. As for which woman you’d rather work for? We’ll leave that up to you.

Can you bring an iced coffee to a job interview?

Cutrone: No. Feeding and hydrating yourself before an interview is always a good idea. This isn’t a casual moment. No one wants a ring stain on their lacquer desk from your matcha bubble tea, or whatever the fuck you’re drinking. No.

Saint John: Yes, you can, but only if you bring one to your interviewer and you know whether or not they’d like coffee. You should email them and ask them. “I’m gonna get an iced coffee. Would you like one? If not, what else can I get you on my way in?”

Is arriving 20 minutes early for an interview punctual or presumptuous?

Cutrone: Presumptuous. People have a schedule for a reason. If you wanna arrive 20 minutes early and stand outside, that’s totally fine. Arrive early, but do it on the DL.

Saint John: That is presumptuous. You don’t know what they’ve got going on. Arrive five minutes ahead of time. Maybe 20 minutes outside the building, and then go up the elevator five minutes before.

Cutrone: Why would you want to embellish your résumé? It’s so easy to find out anything. I think it should be factual. Otherwise it’s fraud, and you’re applying for a job—you’re not there to scam people!

Saint John: You shouldn’t embellish at all, because in this day and age, it can be fact-checked pretty easily. But I do think using beautiful adjectives and great verbs will help to enhance the action that you took or the results that you had in your job. That’s probably as far as the embellishment should go.

Can you carry a Birkin to an interview for an entry-level position?

Cutrone: I wouldn’t. I think it’s a bit of a hyperflex. The first question is: ‘How did you get that Birkin?’ A Birkin is a signal, right? It’s like a stop sign. It reads to the average person like, “I’m rich, and I have good taste." But to people who actually have good taste, they would probably see that as in poor taste. Why do you have to scream it? I wouldn’t necessarily be impressed by that.

Saint John: It depends what the position is. If you are interviewing for a luxury job, then yes, carry the Birkin because maybe they want to see that you have taste, and maybe you should talk about how in the hell you acquired the Birkin on the salary you’re about to receive. But if you’re going in for a job outside the luxury space, keep the Birkin at home. Leave it there.

Is a post-interview thank-you email sufficient, or does a handwritten note still carry weight?

Cutrone: There’s nothing better than a handwritten note. I think there’s something super heartfelt and really chic about a handwritten note. I would do both. Some people don’t really check their emails, and some people don’t check their mailbox. Like, I check my mailbox every two weeks.

Saint John: I think in this day and age, an email is sufficient. A handwritten note depends on the industry. If you’re going for a job in tech, please don’t write a handwritten note. They won’t read it.

Cutrone: Being overfamiliar. Chewing gum. Wearing a ton of piercings—like, for me, it freaks me out. I personally don’t love it. Showing your midriff. Talking poorly about your last employer. Not making eye contact. Speaking in slang.

Saint John: Have questions at the end of the interview! And don’t read them off your phone! Commit them to memory so at least you appear to have been a part of the conversation. And don’t just talk about yourself, my God! Talk about the work, talk about them. Make it a conversation.

Can you leave the office before your boss does?

Cutrone: If I hear the phrase “work-life balance,” it's like a cue to me that this person is not going to make it. It does not exist in the PR or the fashion world. If that’s what you wanna do, you should be a receptionist at a dentist’s office. If somebody who works for me says, “Well, I have Pilates,” that’s not my problem. Why are you scheduling a Pilates class two weeks before fashion week at 6:30 p.m.? If your boss works late, it would be appropriate to say, “Would you like me to stay late and help you take some of this work off your plate?” How about that? What a sweet idea.

Saint John: Yes. You can leave the office before your boss does, but only if it is clearly understood the time you’re expected to be in and out.

Is it acceptable to eat lunch at your desk? What about particularly smelly foods?

Cutrone: Definitely not a good idea. I don’t like to sit next to people who are eating on planes or in my office or anything like that. I don’t wanna smell your cheese, and I don’t like the smell of meat. No one wants to see you eat.

Saint John: You can eat your lunch at your desk if you’re working. But if you are kicking up your feet and relaxed, sipping on your juice and eating your very fragrant food, then you probably should take it outside or take it into the break room. There’s no need to eat your lunch at your desk if you’re not working.

Can you wear pimple patches to the office?

Cutrone: I mean, if you work at Allure. If you’re a biophysicist, maybe. Palo Alto, probably. If I had to make a general statement: No to the pimple patches. I’d say get a facial or get on some sort of program and take care of your skin.

Saint John: I think in this day and age, you can wear pimple patches to the office, but please don’t cover your entire face with them. You should probably go see your doctor if it’s really that bad.

Is crying at your desk acceptable, or should tears be reserved for the bathroom?

Cutrone: Get control of your emotions. If you wanna cry, I get it. Do everything that you can in your power to remove yourself from your coworkers and your work environment. I used to, at People’s Revolution, go in the stairwell to cry. It’s distracting to people, and it’s catchy. People catch vibes in offices.

Saint John: It depends on what you’re crying about. If the tears are about frustration, anger at your boss, or anything that could be misunderstood, then you probably wanna take it to the bathroom and call in one of your closest colleagues to come in there and talk you down. But if the tears are because everybody’s disappointed in the results of the work, I think it’s okay to show the passion.

If your boss asks you to pick up their dry cleaning, can you say it’s not in your job description?

Cutrone: Yes, you can. But when there are six other people there that are waiting to get up through that glass ceiling, you have to assume that when you don’t get the dry cleaning, your name’s not gonna be on your boss’s first pick list. Reciprocation.

Saint John: I think it’s okay to say that, but you probably want to find a diplomatic way to say it. I would ask if they needed help with other things in the office so that they could go pick up their own dry cleaning. Because clearly something else is going on in the office that’s keeping them from running their personal errands.

Are shorts ever appropriate office attire?

Cutrone: It depends on where you work, and it depends on the length of the shorts. Is it a Daisy Duke? If you work at Hooters, I guess yes. (I don’t even know if that’s still open.) If you’re a Dallas Cowboys cheerleader, yeah. It's great! If you work at an insurance company, that’s probably not the best look.

Saint John: Girl, I’ve worked in tech. I’ve seen every kind of workplace attire. Shorts are fine. Just don’t let them be booty shorts. We don’t need to see your cheeks in the office.

How much personal flair should you bring to your office wardrobe?

Cutrone: Work is not a place for you to express yourself. It’s a place for you to express the genetics of the brand or the company that you’re working at. Could you imagine if you went into Louis Vuitton and someone was eating, like, a bowl of Cheerios with big ear gauges in, and was like, “Hey, how y’all doing today?”

Saint John: I once had a well-meaning senior executive tell me not to wear red lipstick or red nail polish to the office because it would signal the wrong thing. I think you should go ahead and do it. Express yourself and make sure that you are also expressing your own personal brand in how you appear. I think that diversity in the workplace is a beautiful thing.

How soon after starting a new job can you go on vacation?

Cutrone: If you work in fashion, you don’t get a vacation between January and March, and you don’t get a vacation from July 15 to October 15. You just don’t. I would say, “I’m so excited to have this job. I’m not in a hurry to go on vacation, but when would be the best time for you for me to plan my vacation?” How’s that for an idea?

Saint John: If you start the job in Q1, you should probably wait until the summer to take a vacation. If you start the job in October, I think you could take off for Christmas and New Year, or the holiday season and New Year, if the office is closed.

Can you use the office printer for personal matters, like printing return labels?

Cutrone: No. Buy a printer with your first paycheck. They cost $149. They’re available at Staples.

Saint John: Absolutely not. Do not use the company printer to print personal things, please. What if you print the wrong thing? Now you’ve got all kinds of personal information out. What if somebody picks it up, you know, mistakenly?

Is it tacky to post about every promotion and professional accomplishment on LinkedIn?

Cutrone: If you’re promoted from an assistant to a junior editor, or from an editor to a director, yes. If you get the bathroom pass for an extra 10 minutes, no.

Saint John: Girl, no. I love that. Go ahead and post every single promotion. I’ve always said that nobody celebrated me until I started celebrating myself. So let the people know you’re doing a great job.

What are your biggest office etiquette no-nos?

Cutrone: Your coworkers aren’t your friends! Do not go out drinking with your coworkers! Pretend your coworkers are your in-laws. Better to say less and do more.

Saint John: Especially in open-concept offices, don’t be too loud! Don't be a disruption to other people around you. Don’t play your music too loud. Don't even talk too loud. If you’re taking personal phone calls, take them outside. Just don’t be a distraction to other people.

Does an 11 p.m. email from your boss warrant an 11 p.m. response?

Cutrone: Technically, no. But most bosses have more than one employee. That boss is gonna remember who responded. If I send an email at 11 o’clock at night, I’m smart enough now to say “for tomorrow,” so my employees don’t have an issue with me from a labor perspective. But I do notice the ones who answer and are like, “Okay, cool, on it, handled,” versus somebody who responds the next morning. The proof is in the pudding. If you’re on a boat in the middle of nowhere, and you have to survive, who are you gonna pick? And that’s what a business is. It’s survival. It’s not a joke.

Saint John: If your boss is asking you about numbers for a deck they need to present in the morning, you should absolutely respond. If your boss is asking you about something that is due a week from now, then you can wait till the morning.

Is a thumbs-up emoji an acceptable response to an email from your boss?

Cutrone: My employee sent me the salute one. I didn’t even know what that was at the beginning. I had to search it in my ChatGPT, like, “What does this mean?” Once I figured it out, I thought to myself, even better than a thumbs-up. I like it because they know I’m running a military organization!

Saint John: Yes, but I would be very careful about the emojis. We know the meanings of different emojis. Please no eggplant emoji. But a heart or a smile, sure. Why not? It’s cute.

Is “per my last email” acceptable or passive-aggressive?

Cutrone: “Per my last email” from somebody who has a lower title than me is not acceptable. I personally, as a boss, prefer, like, “Hey, not sure if you saw this,” or “I’m bumping this up.” But “per my last email” is like, yeah, you’re not following through in the way that you should. I don’t like that vibe.

Saint John: No, that is aggressive. It’s not even passive. Remove the passive. It is aggressive.

Is using ChatGPT to write your emails efficient or just lazy?

Cutrone: I’ve had people with master's degrees in journalism from NYU and Columbia who use ChatGPT. I find it offensive. Like, I didn’t hire you to do that. I hired you because you have a master’s degree in journalism from Columbia. Why don’t you use it? Make your parents proud.

Saint John: At this point, it’s efficient. But you should know the context of the email. You don’t want to respond with ChatGPT, and you’re talking about something that was not in the prior email or not answering the right thing. ChatGPT is not the answer to everything.

Is it acceptable to turn your camera off during a Zoom meeting?

Cutrone: It depends on your title and where you are. If you’re an assistant, some people prefer that. You’re there to take notes, and I think that’s very appropriate and very respectful. That’s something I would ask your boss.

Saint John: Absolutely not. Please turn your camera on. There are so many ways to communicate. It’s not just voice. It’s also expression, and it’s a great way to connect with people. Just make sure you’re communicating the right thing with your face.

Is it acceptable to eat lunch during a Zoom meeting?

Cutrone: Absolutely not. It’s your job to feed yourself. I can’t stand when I’m producing an event with my team, and it’s like 6 p.m. and they go, “We’re hungry.” That’s not my problem. You should have eaten before you came here. Like, feed yourself. I mean, how much food do you actually need? You have a Zoom call at 2 p.m., eat at 1 p.m.. Nobody wants to watch you eat your food. It’s disgusting. So gross.

Saint John: No, we don’t wanna see you chewing. Nobody wants to see you chewing on the Zoom, so please don’t do that. You can wait till after the Zoom to eat, or hurry up and eat and then get on the Zoom.

What are your biggest email and Zoom etiquette no-nos?

Cutrone: Check your background. Blur it out or go on Amazon and get yourself a green screen, because nobody wants to see your Grateful Dead tour T-shirt in your closet. Look chic.

Saint John: Don’t CC everybody on every single email. Only CC the people who are supposed to know, but are not responsible for responding. Don’t just put people in there randomly.

Before You Ship Another Order, Ask If It Could Be a Download

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Digital products skip the shipping, delays and support work that follow every physical order, keeping more profit.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

A customer buys a planner from your store. If it’s paper, the order starts a chain: a supplier packs it, a carrier picks it up and for the next week or two you hope nothing goes wrong.

If it’s a digital planner, the customer pays, gets a download link and starts using it that same evening. Your work on that order is already done.

In my experience with online store owners, I’ve noticed that the part of the business that drains them most is rarely the selling. It’s everything that happens after checkout. Digital products cut most of that out.

Every physical sale comes with a to-do list, even when a supplier handles fulfillment.

You pay for the trip. The supplier charges for shipping on every order, and that fee comes straight out of your margin. Offer free shipping and you either raise the price or absorb the cost yourself.

Your customer waits. Even fast delivery takes days. If there’s a delay, the buyer checks tracking, gets impatient and contacts you. In its 2024 Voice of Customers report, MakesYouLocal analyzed 787,052 support conversations across more than 300 online stores and found that 12% of orders led to a customer service request. The most common question was “Where is my order?”

Things may go wrong. Parcels can get lost, arrive damaged or get stuck in customs. Each case means a refund or reshipment. The bad review usually lands on you, not the carrier and negative reviews can hurt your reputation and scare off new customers.

Cash sits in stock. If you hold inventory, you pay for goods before anyone buys them and then guess how much to reorder. Guess too high and you have to figure out how to sell the leftovers.

Growth means rent and wages. As orders grow, you may need to rent a warehouse or even hire staff to pack them. Rent and salaries are due every month, whether orders come in or not.

Almost nothing. The customer pays, the store sends the file automatically and the order is complete in seconds.

That changes the math. Say you sell a $25 physical product that costs you $8 from the supplier plus $5 for shipping. After a payment fee of about 3%, you keep roughly $11. Now sell a $15 digital guide. After the same fee, you keep about $14.50. The price is lower, the profit is higher and there’s no parcel to track.

It changes the stress level, too. No delayed package, no tracking number to chase, no customer wondering where their purchase went. And every extra copy costs about as much to deliver as the first one: close to nothing, with no warehouse to rent and no packers to pay.

One store owner I’ve worked with, a former psychologist, opened her first online store in 2025. She wanted a business that would take minutes a day, not her whole schedule.

She started with clothing and accessories on a $10-a-day ad budget and got her first four orders the day after launch. Within weeks, she added a few digital guides on self-care and positive thinking, priced as low-cost add-ons.

Over about 14 months, most of her orders have come from small automated ad campaigns. The guide orders were the easiest of all: the buyer paid, the file arrived and there was nothing left to track or explain.

Her daily routine takes 10 to 20 minutes: checking ad reports, processing orders and logging results in a spreadsheet. The guides take none of that time. She didn’t drop physical products. She just let downloads handle the sales that don’t need a box.

Digital products aren’t better at everything. Here’s how the two formats compare on what matters most to a small store.

The biggest risk on the digital side is value. A download has to deliver on its promise from the first sale, because there’s no packaging or unboxing to carry it. Files can also be copied. You can’t fully prevent that, but you can make it less tempting: state license terms on the product page, stamp the buyer’s details on each PDF and cap the number of downloads. Many delivery tools support the last two.

Physical products still win when the object is the point. Nobody downloads a pair of jeans, and many people buy a paper planner precisely to get away from screens.

So is digital better than physical? That’s not really the point. What matters is whether your customer needs the object itself or just what it gives them. Most people who buy a planner, a recipe book or a workout plan don’t care much about the paper. They want the result. If that’s your product, every physical order means extra work you never needed. Try one digital product first. Set it up once, and you’re done. Then keep an eye on your margins, your inbox and your free time.

A customer buys a planner from your store. If it’s paper, the order starts a chain: a supplier packs it, a carrier picks it up and for the next week or two you hope nothing goes wrong.

If it’s a digital planner, the customer pays, gets a download link and starts using it that same evening. Your work on that order is already done.

In my experience with online store owners, I’ve noticed that the part of the business that drains them most is rarely the selling. It’s everything that happens after checkout. Digital products cut most of that out.

World Liberty Financial Proposes WLFI Buybacks as Governance Token Comes Under Pressure

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: World Liberty Financial announced a buyback programme for its WLFI governance token on November 5, with initial funding that may include income from reserves backing its USD1 stablecoin. The company says the plan is intended to support long-term governance participation, but a buyback does not guarantee a token price recovery or reveal how much money will ultimately be spent.

A cryptocurrency buyback sounds reassuring to holders: a project plans to purchase some of its own tokens rather than leave all buying to the market. But the details determine whether that policy creates meaningful change or simply produces another headline.

World Liberty Financial, the crypto venture associated with President Donald Trump and his family, has announced a programme to repurchase WLFI governance tokens. According to reporting on the November 5 announcement, the initiative could draw on revenue from reserves that back World Liberty’s USD1 stablecoin, alongside other sources of funding.

The company says the programme is meant to encourage sustained participation in governance and strengthen voting power among long-term token holders. It has described the arrangement as an ongoing part of its strategy rather than a one-off intervention.

There are important unanswered questions, however. The announcement as reported does not establish a fixed total budget, the precise timetable of purchases or the volume of tokens that will be bought in any particular period. Without those details, investors cannot reliably calculate the potential effect on the circulating supply or market liquidity.

A buyback is also not the same as a token burn. An issuer or affiliated entity might acquire tokens and hold them, distribute them later or subject them to separate governance rules. The effect depends on what happens after purchase and whether the tokens remain able to participate in votes or return to the market.

The reference to stablecoin reserve income warrants particular scrutiny. Reserve earnings, reserves themselves and funds belonging to token holders or users are not interchangeable concepts. The company will need to be clear about the funding pathway if the programme is to be judged on financial substance.

World Liberty Financial was co-founded with involvement from Trump family members and partners. Reuters reported that the family holds a substantial economic interest in the venture, creating an intersection between a politically prominent business and a market in which regulation remains a live issue.

Supporters of the project may view the buyback as an attempt to align incentives and support long-term governance. Critics may question conflicts of interest, disclosure and whether a token with a prominent political connection can be evaluated independently of public office and policy decisions. Both questions deserve examination without assuming that a buyback proves misconduct or that political backing guarantees commercial success.

These governance considerations also extend beyond World Liberty. NewsBTC has covered how custody, market structures and token settlement are changing as institutions enter crypto. A clear governance structure matters when investors are asked to hold tokens whose practical rights differ from ordinary company shares.

The most useful next disclosure would identify the buyback budget, purchases actually completed, average prices and the final treatment of acquired tokens. Regular public reporting would allow holders to compare promises with execution and distinguish token-market operations from the separate stablecoin business.

Price reactions should also be interpreted carefully. A token can rise or fall for reasons unrelated to a buyback, including broader crypto-market conditions and changes in liquidity. Purchases can help support demand at the margin, but they cannot make a token immune to selling or establish its fair value.

For now, World Liberty has announced a policy intention. Whether that intention changes WLFI’s supply dynamics, governance or investor confidence is something future disclosures—not the launch announcement alone—will have to establish.

Review: Queen's Domain (Switch 2) - An Incredible Tribute To FromSoft, And One Of 2026's Best Surprises

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

We heard you liked King's Field, now get ready for...

Way back in the mid-'90s, a business software developer called FromSoftware decided to take a crack at making video games and produced a first-person action fantasy game called King’s Field for the original PlayStation. It did well enough to warrant some sequels, and the studio eventually decided to produce a sort of spiritual successor called Demon’s Souls, a major contributor in solidifying what would come to be known as the Soulslike genre.

There are still lots of fans of that original King’s Field gameplay loop that pine for a return to the roots, and while FromSoft has now moved on to bigger things, the indie scene has stepped up in a major way to deliver us a proper successor. Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain is a retro-fantasy action RPG that masterfully blends tough combat, addictive exploration, and a thrilling sense of mystery to make this a serious contender for the sleeper hit of the year.

The narrative is set in the Kingdom of Bamaen, a once-peaceful place on the brink of destruction after the resurrection of the evil witch Queen Siraatsu. In an attempt to put an end to the calamity, King Abraon and a group of his strongest warriors sailed to the Isle of Neasied to slay the waking queen before she could regain her full strength, but they never returned and the growing onslaught of monsters seems to indicate they failed. You then take on the role of Prince Berium, who shipwrecks upon the isle and sets out in the hopes of uncovering what happened to his father and putting an end to the threat.

After the initial premise is set up, storytelling then takes a backseat and becomes more of a set dressing that you happen to encounter every now and then. Item descriptions and environmental storytelling help to fill in gaps and give more context, but there are precious few moments where cutscenes or major plot beats play out. This approach works well for the kind of atmosphere that Queen’s Domain has in spades—there’s a menacing, haunting aura hanging over the world as you plumb further into its depths, a lot of which comes from the fear of the unknown.

Gameplay could be most closely described as a slower, first-person Soulslike, tasking you with exploring an interconnected, mazelike world riddled with enemies ready to gank you at every turn. You’ll die a lot, but you’ll also learn a lot along the way, and that learning paired with the incremental upgrades that come with combat and exploration leads to a remarkably satisfying ascending power curve.

Queen’s Domain is the kind of game that feels a lot more intimidating up front than it turns out to be and, as with FromSoft's work, it’s that gradual conquest and mastery of its deeper secrets and mechanics that makes it such an excellent and enjoyable experience.

Combat involves a very methodical pace that prioritises careful, strategic moves during the many tense encounters. Especially at first, a few hits can be enough to put you right at death's door, and healing resources are scarce enough that you'll be stuck with most injuries until you make it to the next shrine. The stakes are pretty high, too, given that death has a terrible finality to it. There are no corpse runs here to offer a chance at redemption—if you die, the game simply sends you back to the last time that you saved and erases all progress since.

You can't just hack 'n' slash your way through hordes of foes, and getting jumped by just three at once can sometimes feel like a notable challenge. When hitting back, a small stamina bar represents your total damage output, and taking a swing empties it completely. You then either have to wait for it to refill if you want another full power attack, or you can get in another shot earlier with proportionately weaker strength.

If you want to double down, you can also hold the attack button to ready a charge move that will do increased damage, but this makes the wait even longer and locks you out of other movement and attack options as you helplessly sit there with your weapon raised.

It may sound frustrating, but this leads to richly rewarding combat encounters once you figure out the nuances of your kit. Every weapon has a different range and its own little quirks to get used to. An early favourite of mine was a short-range torch that can be used to rapidly deal passive fire damage and has a limited-use ranged attack that lets you toss a fireball. The more you use a weapon, the more you’ll level it up and increase its damage output, and some weapons evolve into something more advanced if you use them enough.

You’re constantly finding new additions to your kit that fill in interesting niches, and while you can only carry a few of them on you as active weapons, you’re usually pushed to change up your loadout as you move into new areas and come across new enemy types with different weaknesses and damage type resistances.

This gives you lots of opportunities to familiarise yourself with each weapon’s nuances, building lots of muscle memory and mechanical knowledge alongside the raw stat bumps that make enemies drop faster with each increase.

Despite how central combat is to the loop, it feels like you spend even more time simply exploring the world and trying to tease out the correct path forward. Exploration captures that same sense of awe that some of the early Zelda games instilled, as you find yourself cautiously crawling through a dungeon looking for a key to a door you found or trying to figure out what that switch you just triggered unlocked. There’s no map and few signposts pointing you in the right direction, so a lot of your knowledge of a space comes from stumbling around a new area and trying things.

I was pleasantly surprised to find some Metroidvania elements present to add rewards that keep exploration from getting dull. New abilities, such as an air dash or a throwable knife, give you access to previously gated areas, and you’ll sometimes stumble across a consumable item that permanently boosts one of your stats. This goes a long way in keeping a strong and satisfying sense of forward progression, as you can hardly travel in any direction without finding some upgrade or a new route.

Worth a special mention is just how well Freshly Baked Games champions the themes of wonder and mystery throughout its journey. If you're looking at a distant ledge and wondering if you can get there, the odds are pretty good that you not only can, but there's some sort of treat waiting for you. Sometimes you'll stumble down an obscure side path expecting some minimal pickup, only to suddenly enter an entirely new biome that'll take hours to explore.

The thrill and delight that comes with that constant sense of discovery is something that many modern action games struggle to replicate, and — as much as you can — I would encourage you to go into Queen’s Domain blind and experience everything organically. The magic is somewhat lost if you know where to go and what to do to move forward efficiently, and allowing yourself to struggle with frustrations and overcome them by poking around will be immensely more rewarding.

The graphics are strongly inspired by the blocky aesthetics of the PS1, marked by simplistic environments, chunky models, and unfiltered textures that collectively transcend their lo-fi quality to make for a memorable and fitting art style.

And despite the horror themes present in much of the monster and level designs, there’s a fitting amount of levity to be found in occasional goofiness, such as an unlockable duck-themed shop you gain access to after an early boss encounter. Simple though they may be, the visuals help a lot with immersion, as your imagination fills in the gaps and connects you that much more closely with a given sequence.

The only drawback here is mild performance problems—not present enough to negatively affect the moment-to-moment gameplay, but just enough to be noticeable. In areas with more open space or when you’re being assailed by several foes at once, the frame rate will dip for a few seconds before it catches back up. Sometimes distant enemies will also animate at a much lower frame rate to save on some resources, which can make them appear jittery and unnatural.

I wouldn’t say that these performance issues are significant enough to majorly impact your enjoyment, but they are nonetheless disappointing given the rather simplistic visuals.

Despite the often heavy and dark visual themes, the soundtrack usually leans into a more atmospheric style that bizarrely reminded me of Donkey Kong Country and Metroid Prime. Not only does this often contribute to the grand feeling that comes with discovering a new temple or biome, but it gives a slightly positive feeling as you march onward and overcome new challenges. It’s a surprising soundtrack, but it fits very well.

Queen's Domain is one of the best surprises of the year, coming out of nowhere and asserting itself as one of the most satisfying action-adventure games in recent memory.

The rewarding character progression, challenging combat, lo-fi visuals, and thrilling sense of mystery all combine to make this one an easy recommendation to anyone looking for that old-school FromSoft style that recaptures the magic of playing older Zeldas and doesn't hold your hand. It’ll likely win you over if you give it a fair shot.

Making a $300 PS5 Instead of a $1,200 PS6 Would Be 'True Innovation', Says Ex-PlayStation Boss

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

As the PS5 generation approaches its sixth year, talk of what comes next has been circulating for a while, and with hardware as pricey as it is, the prospect of a PS6 isn't as exciting as it perhaps should be.

Opinion differs on that, of course, but it's generally accepted that Sony's next-gen machine is going to be eye-wateringly expensive, and it's currently anyone's guess as to whether it'll be worth a purchase.

Shawn Layden, formerly CEO of PlayStation and head of PlayStation Studios, has weighed in on the matter of next generation console hardware in a new interview.

Speaking on the Trophy Room podcast, he says Sony's best move wouldn't be to create a pricey, brand new console, it would be to revise the current hardware in order to sell it cheaper.

"The typical runway for platforms [is] six to eight years, you get a new one," Layden says. "But typically, that new one brought you something mind-blowing."

He speaks about the various innovative leaps from one console to the next, concluding that the move from PS4 to PS5 was mostly led by raytracing and teraflops. He expects technical aspects like this will be used to sell PS6 as well, but says these are "differences only dogs can hear", implying diminishing returns.

"We have enough power in the machine. The innovation needs to come not from making PS6 for $1200, it's to make another PS5 for $300. Now that would be true innovation."

As Layden points out, the current generation is the first to go up in price rather than down, and he thinks continuing with a PS6 at such a premium price point, and focusing on incremental increases in power, risks alienating the audience.

"What the company needs to do is do what it's never done, which is, don't iterate the console," he says. "Make it smaller, make it cheaper, do all the things you can around that. The silicon is fine, the OS is fine. It's more than enough power to build the kind of games all of us want to play."

While his words of wisdom may ring true for many, Sony itself is forging ahead with its next-gen plans, so we'll likely be faced with a costly new console within the next couple of years.

Bitcoin Drops Below $75K: Key Metric Signals Make-or-Break Support

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Home » Crypto Market News » Bitcoin Drops Below $75K: Key Metric Signals Make-or-Break Support

Shrinking on-chain profits and bearish technicals put Bitcoin’s $74,400 support to the test.

Bitcoin (BTC) price dropped 1.9% over the past 24 hours on Monday, to trade near $74,239, breaking below the psychologically critical $75,000 mark. The decline brings the $74,400 support level into sharp focus as traders weigh the risk of a deeper market correction.

Weakening on-chain profitability and bearish weekly indicators have heightened concerns that BTC could test lower levels toward $70,000.

Derivatives analytics platform CoinGlass recently flagged $74,400 as a key level for BTC, warning on X that a sustained breakdown beneath it would confirm a bearish bias.

With BTC price hovering right near this boundary, buyers must step in to defend the current market structure. A failure to hold $74,400 could open the floodgates for accelerated spot selling.

This is a key level for BTC. A breakdown here would be quite bearish.🤨 pic.twitter.com/q8nmYaQ54G

On-chain analytics reveal a cautious backdrop. Crypto analyst Axel Adler Jr. noted that Bitcoin started the week trading below its one-year median of $77,000.

According to Adler’s analysis, Bitcoin fell 4.4%, from $78K to $74K, between November 1 and 8. Since November 5, it has remained below its 365-day median of $77K, trading between that level and the 25th percentile of $67K.

Adjusted MVRV also declined from 1.075 to 1.047, meaning the average unrealized profit for coins younger than seven years narrowed from 7.5% to 4.7%.

As long as MVRV remains above 1, this group is still in unrealized profit. A drop below 1 would signal unrealized losses.

Adler emphasizes that $77,000 is the key resistance to reclaim. Until Bitcoin regains that median, it remains trapped in a lower range bound by its 25th percentile support at $67,000.

Technical charts align with the cautious on-chain picture. Crypto analyst Ted Pillows pointed on X out that Bitcoin recently closed its fifth consecutive red weekly candle while slipping below its 50-week moving average.

Pillows warned that failing to quickly reclaim this key moving average makes a drop toward $70,000 highly probable—representing an additional 5% to 6% downside from current prices.

$BTC closed its 5th consecutive week in red and also lost the 50W MA.If Bitcoin doesn't reclaim the 50W MA soon, it's highly likely BTC will drop to $70,000. pic.twitter.com/LwcGSkXQoc

Whether Bitcoin price can hold $74,400 and climb back above its $77,000 one-year median will be an important sign of whether selling pressure is easing. If the decline continues and on-chain profitability weakens further, $70,000 could become the next major battleground for bulls.

I regret talking crap about the Ocarina of Time Remake

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The Ocarina of Time remake is out, and we might have an all-timer on our hands. I never doubted Nintendo’s commitment to one of its greatest titles, but I was never sold on its looks, so I made my voice heard. Next time, I’ll let them cook.

I wrote a piece about the Internet’s reception to the Ocarina of Time Remake‘s aesthetics. Lots of people online immediately vibed with what they saw, but a bunch of people were having none of it. It’s even possible that the haters were in the minority, but this is the Internet, and Twitter especially, so negativity will always get the spotlight.

The piece also featured my opinion, and I, despite being pretty much more ok with it than most of the detractors, still wasn’t very keen on the looks. I felt like the game featured an unbecoming mix between fantastical and realistic graphics that I just couldn’t shake. I still don’t love the looks, but I have since learned something very important by looking at the people’s reaction to the actual game.

People are absolutely gushing over it, and it’s not a case of people simply getting their yearly dose of nostalgia. Players, new and old alike, are engaging with it as if it’s this bold new thing that either reignites or creates a completely newfound appreciation for games. That’s the best a game can ever do, both for the player and the industry as a whole.

Though I’ve witnessed accounts of people who’d gotten estranged from games talking about getting pulled back in just because it’s OoT, this is not just a game; this is a very powerful event capable of going from reuniting players with their younger selves to connecting kids with the parents who grew up with the original.

Take a look at the original OoT‘s graphics. They’re far below today’s standard, and since it’s time for me to confess and atone, let me add that I was never too crazy about its colors, which always felt too desaturated for me. I only played it for the first time when I got my GameCube back in ’03 with the famous Wind Waker + OoT bundle and, guess what, as soon as I jumped in, I stopped caring about the graphics altogether.

Graphics are a great selling point, but they’re not what keeps players engaged until the end, especially in an action-adventure title. It’s always going to be the gameplay, and that’s why gamers love higher framerates. Studios might prefer games to look better at the cost of it, because even though the game’s feel while playing dictates how it imprints in your heart and memory, it’s the screenshots that do most of the selling. Sadly, those never show how the game runs and plays.

I have had a bone to pick with remakes for the longest time. Though I believe the first Resident Evil remake, one of the first of its kind, remains one of the finest games of all time, it has mostly just been downhill in the diminishing returns lane since then. I think the best example of what I’m talking about is that of the Resident Evil 4 remake, which adds a neat layer of shiny new paint to one of the greatest games of all time, but forgets most of the superb direction choices that made people fall in love with it for the first time.

At its worst, the Ocarina of Time remake is the exact opposite. It’s a game with graphics that some won’t like, but with a heart so powerful it won’t just make players remember how cool it was to play the original; it will make them feel the kind of childlike wonder they thought they would never encounter again. Or, in short, it’s making us all feel like the Nintendo 64 kid.

Ever been as happy to be gifted a console as that kid? Maybe, if you got a console right about when he did, when 3D games completely shattered our image of what games could be, and the amount of fun they could bring. If you’re a gamer, that was, for a long time, the video that came to mind when you thought about heartwarming vids, because many of us are fortunate to know exactly what that feels like. That’s not the kind of reaction a kid makes simply for being fed the seeds of consumerism; that’s an earnest reaction to encountering a portal to 388 awesome new worlds, or 387, because one of them is Superman 64‘s.

By trashing or amplifying the voices of those talking crap about Ocarina of Time’s new coming before it’s even out, I risked driving people away from ever becoming that kid again, and that’s the worst thing a gamer can legally do. So, if you have the chance to try out Ocarina of Time’s Remake, do it—even if you don’t immediately vibe with its looks.

Tiago is a freelancer who used to write about video games, cults, and video game cults. He now writes for Destructoid in an attempt to find himself on the winning side when the robot uprising comes.

Topics covered: Video editing, photography, photo editing, English Literature, World Cinema, International Politics, Video Game History, Comedy Writing

Alisson reveals future ambitions with Liverpool contract set to expire

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The Brazilian goalkeeper, 34, has won every major club honour during his time at Anfield

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Alisson insists he wants to stay at Liverpool beyond the end of his current contract, which is due to expire next summer.

The 34-year-old has won every major club honour since arriving at Anfield in the summer of 2018 following a £67m deal from Roma. In the eight years since, Alisson has developed into one of the best goalkeepers in the world.

Yet the Brazilian’s current deal expires at the end of the current season but, speaking after making several impressive saves in the 3-1 win at Crystal Palace on Sunday, Alisson insists he wants to stay at Liverpool and “at the top level” for a while yet. His agent has begun negotiations with the club about an extension.

Asked if he wants to stay at the club, Alisson replied: “Yes, in my mind. I’m focused on playing every game but in my mind I’m happy to stay. You know, that depends on a lot of aspects and different situations.

“But my will is to stay. I’m happy here, my family is happy here. I’m still hungry for titles and for delivering good performances. I still want to play at the top level for as long as I can. So that’s my thinking on that.”

While Alisson has been the No 1 since joining the club, Giorgi Mamardashvili is a top-tier back-up and the Reds have also signed youngster Lucca Brughmans from Genk for £23.8m. The 18-year-old Belgian will join from the 2027-28 season.

Meanwhile, Alisson was not called up by Brazil boss Carlo Ancelotti for the upcoming friendlies against Japan and Singapore, insisting “we don’t need to test him.” Nonetheless, the keeper acknowledged it was a call taken “against his will.”

He added: “It is beneficial (not going), for sure. You have to use it right because you have a lot of time so if you don’t train, if you don’t prepare, time goes really quick and you need to stay sharp.

“It’s not that I will sit on the couch for a week and then come back training. I still have to work hard but in my mind I can use the time to relax and be more with my family.

“It’s a little bit against my will. What I always want is to be with the national team but I understand this is what I have at the moment and I will use this time and take the benefit from the rest.”

Liverpool head into the international break fourth in the Premier League table, nine points off leaders Arsenal.

Meta’s Dave Wehner leaves to become CFO of AI start-up Fireworks

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Dave Wehner will run the AI inference start-up’s finances as it prepares a new round. Reports put its next valuation at $30bn or more, against $17.5bn in July.

Dave Wehner has spent 14 years at Meta, more than eight of them as chief financial officer. On Friday, he announced that he is leaving his role as chief strategy officer to become CFO of Fireworks, an AI inference start-up.

The Information first reported the move on Friday evening. Wehner joined Facebook in 2012 as vice president of corporate finance and business planning. He became CFO in 2014 and moved to the strategy role in 2022.

“Fireworks has the right mission for the moment,” Wehner wrote on X.

In the post, he thanked Mark Zuckerberg and said he was “incredibly optimistic” about Meta’s future. He said he had known Fireworks chief executive Lin Qiao for a long time and wanted to join a founder-led growth company.

“I see AI as the most consequential technology shift of my lifetime. I’m excited to get to work and help build a business at the center of it,” he wrote.

Lin told CNBC that Wehner will scale the company’s finances and operations. She also wrote about the hire on LinkedIn on Friday.

“Dave told me he wanted to get back to a smaller company where he could build toward a big impact again,” Lin wrote.

Meta chief operating officer Javier Olivan also gave CNBC a statement.

“Dave helped guide Meta through tremendous growth and complexity over 14 years, and brought disciplined thinking to some of our hardest company-level decisions,” Olivan said.

Former Meta and Google employees founded Fireworks in 2022. Its platform lets developers customise and run open AI models for their businesses. It raised $1.5bn in July at a $17.5bn valuation, in a round led by Atreides Management, Index Ventures and TCV. Nvidia and Sequoia Capital are also backers.

Two outlets have reported on its next round. Business Insider reported in October that it was in early talks to raise at a valuation of $30bn to $40bn. The Information now reports that Fireworks will start a new round next week, citing people familiar with the matter. It is targeting a valuation of at least $30bn, The Information reported. TNW has not independently verified either report.

Other inference start-ups have also raised big rounds in recent weeks, including Fal at $16bn and Baseten’s planned round.

Before Facebook, Wehner was CFO of Zynga and a managing director at the investment bank Allen & Co., The Information reported. He also sits on the board of Aurora Innovation, which makes self-driving technology for trucks.

Electric dirt bikes keep getting called e-bikes. The bicycle industry wants that to stop

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The line between electric bicycles and electric motorcycles has been getting increasingly blurry – though perhaps not because the vehicles themselves are actually that hard to tell apart.

Instead, much of the confusion comes down to terminology. High-powered electric dirt bikes capable of reaching motorcycle-like speeds are increasingly being referred to as “e-bikes,” despite sharing relatively little with the low-speed electric bicycles millions of Americans use for commuting and recreation.

Now the U.S. bicycle industry is asking federal regulators to make that distinction much clearer.

PeopleForBikes, the bicycle industry’s leading trade association, recently testified before the U.S. Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC), calling for stronger federal regulations that specifically distinguish electric bicycles from higher-powered e-motos and electric dirt bikes.

“E-bikes and e-motos are fundamentally different products, and federal safety regulations need to reflect that distinction,” explained PeopleForBikes Policy and General Counsel Matt Moore. “When high-powered electric dirt bikes are marketed as e-bikes, it creates confusion for consumers and puts the credibility of legitimate e-bikes at risk.”

There’s a real nomenclature problem hiding underneath all of this. The term “e-bike” is widely used as shorthand for “electric bicycle,” but it isn’t exclusively used that way. Motorcycles have been called “bikes” for generations, and motorcycle media and riders will sometimes casually refer to electric motorcycles as “e-bikes” too, just in the way a biker refers to his Harley as his “bike”. In fact, it all comes from the same root. We don’t call motorcyclists “motorists”, do we? No… we call them “bikers”.

All of that makes phrases like “legal e-bike” and “legitimate e-bike” that were even used by PeopleForBikes in its arguments somewhat problematic when discussing proposed regulation. The term “e-bike” simply doesn’t mean anything specific, at least not legally.

What actually matters from a legal standpoint is whether a vehicle meets the codified definition of an electric bicycle, not whether someone calls it an e-bike – especially when the term e-bike is practically applied so broadly. In other words, perhaps part of separating electric bicycles from e-motos is being more precise with the terminology ourselves: “electric bicycle” has a specific regulatory meaning, while “e-bike” has increasingly become a much fuzzier colloquial term. As much as some would want the term “e-bike” to mean something with pedals, it simply doesn’t – at least not in North America.

I’m certainly guilty of this myself in my writing and videos, falling back on the commonly used phrasing instead of the technical legal terminology, but keeping them separated could help with the growing misunderstanding in the industry.

That distinction has become increasingly important as lightweight electric motorcycles and dirt bikes have exploded in popularity. Many feature bicycle-like frames and components, and some even include pedals, but their performance can extend far beyond what federal law defines as a low-speed electric bicycle.

The concern goes beyond mere semantics. When a 50-60 mph (80-100 km/h) electric dirt bike gets lumped into the same category as a 20-28 mph (32-45 km/h) commuter e-bike, crashes and reckless riding involving the former can quickly become part of the public conversation surrounding the latter. That can ultimately influence how lawmakers, regulators, police departments, and the general public view e-bikes as a whole.

In fact, many of the supposed electric bicycle crash figures we’ve seen touted in the news aren’t from electric bicycles at all, but rather from e-moto accidents that have been lumped in with electric bicycles, making the latter appear more dangerous than they truly are.

If street-legal electric bicycles were separated from e-motos in crash statistics, the category would be revealed to be much safer than conventional reporting would have us believe.

All of this and more has led to PeopleForBikes pushing federal regulators to draw a clearer line.

As part of its CPSC testimony, the organization recommended retaining the existing federal definition of a low-speed electric bicycle while creating a separate definition for e-motos, including machines capable of speeds up to 60 mph (96 km/h). It also called for mandatory electrical safety standards for off-road e-motos, which it says currently fall into a regulatory gap, as well as greater scrutiny of conversion kits capable of turning conventional bicycles into vehicles operating beyond their original structural design limits.

The testimony came as the CPSC considers new mandatory federal battery safety standards for e-bikes and other electric mobility devices. PeopleForBikes says it supports stronger battery standards, while recommending that regulators rely on existing UL standards without modification.

PeopleForBikes is also asking the CPSC and National Highway Traffic Safety Administration to coordinate enforcement against e-motos that evade federal requirements.

None of this means e-motos are inherently bad. They’re incredibly fun machines, and there’s a rapidly growing market for them. But a 60 mph (96 km/h) electric dirt bike isn’t an electric bicycle simply because it looks vaguely like one.

And increasingly, the bicycle industry wants regulators to make sure everyone understands the difference.

Teens and parents can expect Instagram notifications about AI age estimation

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Meta says it’s expanding its AI age estimation globally to automatically extend Instagram account protections to millions more teenagers. It says parents in the US can also expect to receive notifications later this month …

Instagram has a number of protections for the accounts of teenagers, but it’s very common for teens to lie about their age in order to circumvent them. Back in May, the company said it would be using AI-powered age estimation to address the problem.

The company said it would use the tool to block users aged below 13, and to ensure that those in the 13 to 17 range had limited accounts. Teen accounts have age-appropriate feeds, limitations on who can interact with them, and default time management tools – though things didn’t get off to the best of starts when a 12-year-old kid fooled it by using an eyebrow pencil to draw a fake moustache.

Meta today says that it is rolling out the (hopefully now eyebrow pencil-proof) tech globally.

We use AI technology to find people we suspect to be teens, even if they use an adult birthday, and place them in Teen Account protections. Since first introducing this technology, we’ve been placing more teens in age-appropriate protections and are encouraged by the results. Based on that success, today we’re expanding this technology globally.

The company goes on to say that parents in the US will receive a notification sometime this month, with a link to a video and an article about the system.

This month, parents in the United States will begin to see an in-feed video on Instagram explaining our approach to ensuring their teens are in age-appropriate protections. The video will link to an article, written by Larry Magid, internet safety advocate and President and CEO of ConnectSafely, detailing our multi-layered age assurance approach, which includes keeping children under 13 off our platforms, providing age appropriate experiences for teens, and informing parents about ways they can partner with us to ensure their teens provide the correct age online.

If you don’t want to wait, you can read the article here.

Meta says the same protections will be rolled out to Threads and Facebook, although I would have thought being on Facebook in the first place is proof that it’s been a while since you were a teenager …

Infant Bleeding Cases Surge as More Parents Refuse Vitamin K Shots for Newborns

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Two large studies published on November 5 link declining newborn vitamin K shot rates to a sharp increase in infant bleeding cases, including potentially fatal brain hemorrhages.

A research letter, published in JAMA Pediatrics, analyzed data on approximately 11 million infants from 2017 to 2025 and found that vitamin K administration declined from 97.7% to 95%, while cases of vitamin K deficiency bleeding (VKDB) nearly quadrupled.

A second study published in JAMA, based on data from 5.2 million U.S. infants, found that refusal rates more than doubled, rising from 3.1% in 2017 to 6.8% in 2025. Infants who did not receive the shot had a 47% higher risk of bleeding and more than twice the risk of intracranial bleeding.

Additionally, new data from Truveta suggest the trend is continuing: refusals of vitamin K shots increased by 57% between January and June 2026, rising from 5.2% to 8.1% of newborns.

Vitamin K is essential for blood clotting, and newborns naturally have very low levels. Without the preventive injection, infants are at risk for VKDB, which can cause sudden bleeding in the brain, gastrointestinal tract, and other organs. Late-onset VKDB can occur up to six months after birth, often without warning signs, and may be fatal.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) continue to recommend an intramuscular vitamin K injection for all newborns, ideally within 6 hours of birth.

The shot is not a vaccine; it is a nutrient injection that helps prevent dangerous bleeding. A single dose reduces the risk of late-onset VKDB by approximately 98%.

Experts not involved in the studies attribute the rise in refusals partly to broader vaccine hesitancy and mixed public health messaging.

“The decision to vaccinate a newborn or allow a vitamin K injection at birth is complex, with multiple factors playing a role in the ultimate decision,” said Graham Tse, MD, a pediatrician and chief medical officer of MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, CA.

Some of these factors, Tse said, include conflicting messages from federal health agencies such as the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), both overseen by HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr., an outspoken critic of the childhood immunization schedule.

The vitamin K shot is not a vaccine, yet RFK Jr. has declined to reassure parents about its safety, despite the CDC’s current recommendation to administer it at birth.

Additionally, growing medical distrust among parents has led to refusal of routine newborn care, such as the vitamin K shot, Reuters reports.

Healthline spoke with Tse to learn more about the risks of refusing the vitamin K shot and what new or expectant parents should know about this routine newborn treatment.

This interview has been lightly edited and condensed for clarity and length.

Tse: The decision to vaccinate a newborn or allow a vitamin K injection at birth is complex, with multiple factors playing a role in the ultimate decision.

A contributing factor to the decline in vitamin K injection at birth, as well as in newborn hepatitis B vaccination, is the conflicting messages from the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

Recommendations from these entities and others are conflicting or do not align with established, research-based, and proven recommendations from organizations such as the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) and the Infectious Diseases Society of America.

When there are conflicting recommendations, it creates confusion and doubt among parents about what the best or right choice is for their child.

As well, there is so much misinformation on the internet, among influencers, and on social media that it is difficult to know what information is accurate, well-researched, and proven, versus what may be just someone’s opinion or belief.

Some are mistaking the vitamin K injection at birth for a vaccination, which it is not. Ultimately, all these factors are contributing to parents declining or delaying vitamin K injections at birth.

Tse: Declining the newborn vitamin K injection puts the newborn at significant risk of serious and life threatening complications from vitamin K-deficient bleeding (VKDB). This was previously referred to as hemorrhagic disease of the newborn.

VKDB can be as mild as bruising of the skin but can be as serious as bleeding into the brain, and newborns and infants can die from VKDB.

Declining vitamin K injection at birth is significant because of the serious and potentially life threatening consequences of deficiency of Vitamin K and not being able to clot the blood.

The vitamin K injection at birth is highly effective at preventing VKDB.

Tse: If a neonate or infant has vitamin K deficiency, the ability to clot their blood and stop bleeding is highly reduced.

All newborns are at risk of low vitamin K due to low body stores, low vitamin K content of breast milk, reduced ability of the gut to mobilize vitamin K, and poor transfer of vitamin K across the placenta.

When the blood cannot clot, bleeding from minor or minimal trauma can occur, and even bleeding without injury or trauma may occur. This can include bruises, bleeding from the mouth or gastrointestinal tract, bleeding from cuts or circumcision.

Bleeding can occur anywhere in the body, including in the brain, which can be catastrophic.

VKDB can occur from birth and through several months of life. Bleeding in the brain from VKDB, one of the most serious consequences, can occur at any time from birth to up to 6 months or so and can occur unexpectedly and suddenly. Vitamin K given at birth can prevent this risk of bleeding.

Tse: Vitamin K is a necessary vitamin that all humans need. Vitamin K is not a vaccination.

Newborns need vitamin K to prevent serious, significant, and often catastrophic bleeding during their first several months of life.

Vitamin K has been given to newborns in the United States for close to 70 years, is safe, is studied, and is proven to help prevent vitamin K-deficient bleeding.

I urge new parents to allow their newborns to receive the vitamin K injection at birth — it protects them, keeps them healthy, is proven safe, and is recommended.
